<!-- p. 33 -->

The membrane analysis of thin masonry shells

Jacques Heyman

The stresses in masonry are usually very low compared with the basic strength of the material.
Thus the analysis of a masonry structure is concerned primarily with the determination of a pattern
of forces to equilibrate the known loading, and hence to estimate the required strength of supports
– to find, for example, the thrust of a high vault, and hence the values of the loads imposed on the
buttressing system. This paper is concerned with the use of the equations of membrane theory to
analyse such vaults.

These equations are concerned essentially with statements of equilibrium, and make no reference
to material properties or to deformations; any imposed boundary conditions are concerned with
loads rather than displacements. The paper is entirely non-mathematical, and examines
descriptively the basic second-order partial differential equation. This equation may appear in one
of two fundamentally different forms. If the thin vault – the shell – has positive Gaussian curvature
(for example, a dome), then irregularities (for example, an anomalous point load) diffuse quickly
within the shell. If on the other hand the Gaussian curvature is negative (a saddle, a Candela
hyperbolic paraboloid, a fan vault), then a point load can (in theory) propagate unchanged through
the shell.

Membrane theory assumes that a shell has infinitesimal thickness, and solutions for a prescribed
geometry – a hemispherical dome for example – may lead inexorably to the development of tensile
stress resultants within the shell. Such tensile stresses are inadmissible for a masonry structure,
and the solutions must be modified to allow for small but real finite thicknesses. Such restrictions
on the use of membrane theory are discussed, but it is concluded that the theory gives good
primary estimates in the analysis of a practical structure. In particular, they indicate that a masonry
dome can be relatively simple to construct, whereas a fan vault requires elaborate centering to
support the masonry until a whole bay is complete.

Keywords: masonry; thin vaults; construction; structural analysis; membrane theory

Volte “tabicadas” nelle grandi isole del Mediterraneo: Sicilia e Sardegna (XV-XVIII secolo)

Maria Mercedes Bares -Marco Rosario Nobile

Allo stato attuale delle ricerche le volte “tabicadas” costituiscono per la Sicilia e la Sardegna una
scoperta recente, troppo episodici risultano ancora i casi conosciuti. Paradossalmente sono le
fabbriche più precoci, tutte in Sicilia (torre di Pozzallo, castello di Butera, convento di Santa Maria
del Gesù a Modica), quelle che presentano minori problemi storiografici, poiché direttamente
collegabili a committenti catalani e quindi a esperienze costruttive di importazione. Più problematici
appaiono gli esempi riscontrabili in importanti edifici civili della Palermo del Settecento e
individuabili anche attraverso le fonti notarili. Altrettanto si può dire per i cosiddetti “ ladrillos de
boveda” documentati in Sardegna. L’ipotesi più plausibile rimane quella di una tecnica costruttiva
veicolata da esperti esterni, probabilmente ingegneri militari.
